##### **Order-Consistent Latent Geometry for Reliable Short-Horizon Planning in World Models**
2610.18570v1 by Junlin Wu, Wen Fan, Dandan Zhang

Latent planners often rank candidate action sequences by terminal Euclidean distance to a goal latent. Exact agreement with transition distance would make this score ideal, but is stronger than planning requires: along a transition-shortest rollout, the essential requirement is that latent distance does not reverse true transition progress. We show that this pathwise no-reversal requirement is precisely the classical self-approaching condition. To the best of our knowledge, we are the first to identify self-approachingness as a planner-facing compatibility condition for terminal Euclidean latent planning and to connect its violation to an exact finite-horizon candidate-order reversal. Because visual world models predict discrete latent sequences, we analyze their polygonal interpolation. We prove that an acute bend creates a nonempty region in which terminal Euclidean distance ranks a transition-worse candidate ahead of one that makes genuine progress, even under exact rollouts. We further interpret latent dimension as angular capacity through a classical spherical-code bound. A controlled six-branch experiment, including a zero-padded three-dimensional baseline, validates the predicted ordering failure and separates angular arrangement from ambient dimensionality.

##### **Rethinking Quality in Large Language Model Routing: Security-Conditioned Model-and-Budget Selection**
2610.18566v1 by Yichi Zhang, Zhenxiang Huang, Yuyang Zhang, Yuchen Yang

Large language model (LLM) routers reduce serving cost by choosing which model to call and how many output tokens to grant it for each query. Existing routers rank model-budget actions using a single quality metric. This does not work for mixed workloads. For ordinary queries, a good answer is correct and useful (utility); for security-sensitive queries, a good answer refuses harmful requests or resists malicious instructions (security). These two objectives prefer different models and budgets.
  We propose SCRouter, a router that learns when to prioritize security and how to allocate the token budget. For each model-budget pair, it estimates the probability that the response finishes within budget. It uses this probability to predict utility and security separately. A small probe determines how to weight these two predictions for each query. SCRouter then selects the model and budget by balancing the weighted utility and security against cost. We also build SCRouterBench, a benchmark of 38,869 utility and security queries spanning nine security dimensions, each answered under all 144 combinations of 12 models and 12 budgets, plus 2,753 out-of-domain queries. SCRouter improves the baselines by 13-29 points in-domain and 19-23 points out-of-domain.

##### **Input-Conditioned Search for Efficient Test-Time Scaling of Recursive Reasoners**
2610.18547v1 by Haochen Shi, Tianwen Fu, Qianggang Ding, Yinhan Lu, Yizhan Li, Huan Zhang, Yajie Zhao, Xingdi Yuan, Hengyang Lu, Bang Liu

People often tackle difficult problems by thinking more deeply or exploring more possibilities. Similarly, recursive reasoners, which reason by repeatedly applying a learned update rule to latent states, can also scale inference through more updates (depth) or additional trajectories generated by perturbing latent states (width). When people hit a dead end in their thinking, they may step back and approach the problem from a fresh angle. However, neither depth scaling nor perturbation-based width scaling of recursive reasoners can reliably achieve this: both may converge to incorrect attractors. To address this, we propose to explore diverse reasoning traces by applying different update maps to the same latent state, without any additional training. We realize this idea through \emph{Input-Conditioned Search (ICS)}, which searches over input conditions to induce alternative update maps from the pretrained reasoner. Across six reasoning tasks, ICS achieves the highest mean accuracy, while requiring up to two orders of magnitude fewer model FLOPs than the evaluated scaling baselines. Controlled experiments confirm that ICS can redirect trajectories from the same state. Our theoretical analysis and rigorous numerical verification also confirm that ICS can locally eliminate incorrect attracting fixed points. Together, these findings establish ICS as an effective axis for efficient test-time scaling and motivate further research on shaping recursive reasoning during training and at test time.

##### **Retrieval Disagreement as a Signal: Diagnosing and Steering Multilingual Dense Embedders**
2610.18526v1 by Faeze Ghorbanpour, Shaghayegh Kolli, Jana Diesner, Alexander Fraser

Multilingual dense retrievers place all languages in one shared space so that a question finds relevant documents, regardless of which language they are written in. We show that the same question, asked in different languages, retrieves substantially different documents, and this disagreement is not because of weak language understanding. Each language performs well on its own and ranks candidates consistently with the other languages; divergence appears only when languages share an index. We trace the mechanism: each language keeps its own ranking, but in the shared index, its correct answers are pushed far down, buried under non-relevant documents that are either in the query's own language or in English, depending on the model. We introduce disagreement-guided steering: for each language, we contrast the documents that other languages agree on for a question against those that language retrieves idiosyncratically, and take the difference of their representations as a language-specific direction, added to that language's queries at inference. ##### **Lost in Implicit Routing: How LLM Agents Fail to Load Relevant Skills at Scale**
2610.18522v1 by Jinlin Liu, Wei Chen, Xiaojin Zhang

Reusable skills let LLM agents load task-specific instructions on demand, but they make execution depend on implicit routing over skill metadata. With 1,000 irrelevant skills, loaded-skill recall falls from 73.6% to 27.4% and the zero-load rate rises from 9.3% to 51.6%, whereas explicit selection from the same library retains 73.9% recall. 2610.18520v1 by Swathi Shree Narashiman, Aravind Ramana Venkatramanan, Nambi Seshadri

  \[ z_{(r)} \approx μ- α\log r. \] across prediction contexts, architectures, and scales, emerging progressively during pretraining. To our knowledge, this is the first systematic analysis of the logit geometry underlying the distributional assumptions used in the prior decoding work. While controlled order-statistic baselines spanning bounded, light-tailed, and heavier-tailed distributions can largely explain the observed log-linear geometry, they do not explain the systematic shift in the slope $α$: trained language models exhibit $α\approx 1.4$--$1.7$, sitting well above the initialization baseline ($α\approx 0.2$) . ##### **Imprinting: Knowledge Distillation Can Shift Student Behavior**
2610.18517v1 by Yu-Chien Tang, An-Zi Yen

Knowledge distillation trains student models on teacher-generated responses to transfer task capability, but those responses may also carry behavioral tendencies of their source. Students generally acquire task capability while their downstream decisions shift toward the source teacher's safety-pragmatism disposition, a phenomenon we call imprinting. Distillation from the harm-accepting teacher reduces harm avoidance, whereas distillation from the harm-avoiding teacher increases it. Imprinting varies across teacher-student pairs and does not extend uniformly across different forms of safety behavior. Excluding a sparse set of divergence tokens reduces imprinting, whereas response rewriting preserves the source-dependent effect and can introduce additional rewriter-dependent shifts. To address these limitations, we propose \textbf{MemoryVLN}, a unified memory-driven navigation system built around memory construction and management. During memory construction, \emph{Long Sequence Compression} employs forgetting-curve-inspired temporal sampling to preserve dense recent evidence and sparse yet representative distant observations under a limited token budget. \emph{Ego-Aware Memory Representation} further integrates 2D semantic tokens, 3D geometric tokens, and 6D agent poses, allowing each memory to encode both what was observed and where it was observed. During memory management, \emph{Hierarchical Memory Bank} maintains in-episode and context-level memories through cross-context warm-start and confidence-controlled write-back. \emph{Memory Enhancement} then retrieves the Top-$K$ relevant memories according to the current navigation state and fuses them with the current visual tokens. Together, these mechanisms transform raw navigation history into compact, spatially grounded, selectively retrievable, and reusable navigation experience. Extensive experiments on R2R-CE and RxR-CE demonstrate consistent improvements over the state-of-the-art baselines by a substantial margin (i.e., 2\% and 6.5\% in successful rate).

##### **How Safe Is ChatGPT Health Advice? Evidence from Real User Conversations in Four Global South Countries**
2610.18465v1 by Shreyasi Roy Chowdhury, Kiran Garimella

People increasingly bring their health questions to ChatGPT, yet safety evaluations of LLM health advice rest almost entirely on clinician-authored vignettes, synthetic prompts, or simulated user profiles. The gap is widest for the Global South, where concerns about demographic bias and unsafe recommendations are well documented but have never been tested on real usage. We present, to our knowledge, the first demographically grounded safety evaluation of real-world health conversations with ChatGPT. From a consent-based data-donation corpus of 202,590 conversations from 1,252 users in India, Nigeria, Brazil, and Pakistan (December 2022 to February 2026), each linked to self-reported age and gender, we identify 15,555 health conversations, classify their health intent using an LLM classifier adapted from an established taxonomy, and score response safety under both a context-blind and a user-context-aware rubric. Roughly one in five health conversations receives a high-risk rating. Risk concentrates in symptom and condition queries and recurs through identifiable failure modes: unsafe medication or dosage guidance, harmful self-diagnosis, missing safety warnings, encouraging over-reliance on the model, and failure to refer users to professionals. Supplying the user's own accumulated context raises the high-risk share by 13-17 percentage points, and about one in four conversations rated safe in isolation turns out to be unsafe for the specific person asking. Safety evaluations built on synthetic cases are likely to miss these harms.

##### **Readout Scaling Shapes Sparse Feature Recovery**
2610.18462v1 by Brain Xiao, Sarah Wilson

Readout scaling changes what a sparse autoencoder (SAE) learns, but the benefit of removing it depends on the data. We use readout-canonicalized decision-space (RC-DS) coordinates to remove singular-value gains while preserving retained decision information. At a fixed readout condition number, changing the generating distribution reverses which training coordinates recover known factors best. In our controlled coordinate-matching study, recovery is highest when training restores the space in which the factors were generated. When factors are generated before readout scaling, RC-DS increases mean sparse-factor recovery from 0.506 to 0.913 at condition number 100, compared with centered-logit training under the same budget. An exact wrapper reproduces RC-DS training to numerical precision, isolating training geometry rather than additional information. In neural policies, RC-DS improves mean logit reconstruction under the tested budgets. On three fixed language-model checkpoints, RC-DS has higher mean gain-free reconstruction in all 18 tested task-sparsity settings, while centered-logit SAEs reconstruct output logits better in 17. Small-label-head controls show that longer training reduces the reconstruction gap from 0.0198 to 0.0016. Together, these results show how readout scaling, the data distribution, and training shape sparse learning. RC-DS provides an information-preserving method for studying and controlling the readout's contribution. Separate label-detection and steering tests distinguish these reconstruction effects from task-specific feature usefulness.

##### **Teacher-Guided Tool Learning for Small Language Models via Bidirectional KL Constraints**
2610.18459v1 by Haoxuan Gao, Qin-Wen Luo, Xiang Chen, Yu Tian, Zequn Sun, Xiaofeng Cao, Jiangchao Yao, Sheng-Jun Huang

External tools can extend the capabilities of small language models (SLMs), enabling their deployment in resource constrained environments. However, their limited capabilities make it difficult for SLMs to discover effective tool use behaviors through reinforcement learning alone, while direct teacher supervision suffers from teacher student behavioral distribution mismatch and may propagate erroneous preferences. To address these challenges, we propose Selective Teacher guided Policy Optimization (SeTPO), which coordinates student exploration with selective teacher guidance and persistent demonstration supervision. Specifically, SeTPO incorporates reverse KL derived token level teacher feedback into policy advantages on low reward trajectories, while auxiliary forward KL supervision preserves teacher demonstrated behaviors throughout training. Under an idealized bidirectional KL objective, we theoretically establish that the optimal policy achieves expected reward no lower than the teacher's while retaining a guaranteed fraction of the teacher's probability mass. Extensive experiments on BFCL v4 and API Bank demonstrate that SeTPO achieves superior overall performance over supervised fine tuning, reinforcement learning, and distillation baselines across all three student scales.

##### **Autonomous Science Bench: Evaluating AI Agents for Autonomous Scientific Discovery**
2610.18449v1 by Yibo Wen, Han Liu

AI agents for autonomous scientific discovery must decide which experiments to run under a limited budget. Most benchmarks for science agents provide fixed datasets, while others that support active experimentation largely rely on simulation. We introduce Autonomous Science Bench, an initial release comprising six budgeted design-build-test-learn campaigns grounded in published experiments across biology, chemistry, and materials science. Agents select batches of designs, receive measurements from a lab service, and use the results to guide subsequent experiments before delivering a predictive model. Success requires passing two calibrated gates: a discovery gate, which evaluates the designs the agent measured, and a learning gate, which evaluates its model's ranking of held-out designs. In a pilot evaluation of eight agent-model configurations and 144 trials, the best configuration succeeds in 67% of trials, while the hardest campaign is solved in only three of 24 attempts. The two gates reveal different weaknesses: some agents build accurate rankings but find too few promising designs, while others find promising designs but fail to predict which unseen designs will perform well. These differences are obscured by overall success rates alone. We release version 0.1 of the campaigns, lab runtime, and public leaderboard at https://yibow.me/autonomous-science-bench.

##### **Thinker-WM: A Unified World Model with Shared Multimodal Representations for Embodied Intelligence**
2610.18447v1 by  Thinker Team, Peng Gao, Jichao Jiao, Chen Liao, Daqin Luo, Yiming Ma, Baiyu Pan, Hailin Shi, Jiyuan Wang, Xiangming Xu, Xiongwei Zhao

World models have emerged as a promising paradigm for embodied AI, yet existing systems often remain fragmented in the modalities they model and the functions they support. In particular, visual dynamics, geometric structure, and executable action are commonly coupled through separate interfaces or downstream modules rather than being jointly represented and evolved within a shared dynamics model. In this work, we present Thinker-WM, a unified multimodal world model that jointly models and generates RGB, metric depth, and robot action through a shared intermediate Unified Dynamics Transformer with modality-specific encoders and decoders. The model performs multimodal unification at the representation level: appearance, geometry, and action are transformed into a common dynamic representation and jointly evolved by shared parameters, while their distinct output structures are preserved. The same parameterization supports multiple functions, including joint video-action generation, world prediction, policy inference, action-conditioned generation, video-to-action generation, and inverse dynamics. To examine whether the shared representation captures action-related information, we introduce a lightweight motion probe that shows the frozen visual representation contains recoverable motion information. Thinker-WM is trained on a heterogeneous corpus comprising approximately 20K hours of egocentric video, 10K hours of real-robot data, and 1K hours of simulation data, including approximately 4K hours of real-robot data with aligned depth supervision. Extensive experiments demonstrate strong performance across embodied world-model evaluations. Thinker-WM ranks first on EWMBench, WorldOlympiad and WorldArena, while controlled studies further demonstrate the benefits of action conditioning, geometric interaction, and multi-function training within the shared representation.

##### **The Singularity Has a Distribution: Exact Timing Laws and Detection Limits for a Stochastic Intelligence Explosion**
2610.18445v1 by Xiaoyu Li, Zheng Gao, Chiwun Yang, Zhizhou Sha

Automating AI research creates a positive feedback loop between software capability and research effort. We study a stochastic realisation of this loop in which independent, positive, multiplicative improvements arrive at a rate proportional to a power of current capability. Within this model, randomness leaves the deterministic explosion threshold unchanged but replaces the blow-up date by an explicit distribution. For equal-sized improvements the explosion time is a geometrically weighted sum of independent exponentials; its $q$-series representation gives its transforms, moments and tail behaviour, including a two-term small-time asymptotic. For Lomax-distributed improvement sizes the explosion time is exactly Gamma distributed. Self-similarity implies that, with parameters known, the relative uncertainty about the remaining time does not vanish as explosion approaches. We formulate a fixed-sample test between constant and geometrically increasing idea-arrival rates. If the baseline rate and the alternative log-rate increment $\ell$ are known, the minimum sample size at error level $α$ is asymptotic to $(12z_α^2)^{1/3}\ell^{-2/3}$, where $z_α$ is the upper-$α$ standard normal quantile. Uncertainty about the growth exponent near its critical value produces a $1/t$ tail with an explicit constant, and hence an infinite conditional mean waiting time, although explosion is certain given a positive exponent. Separate extensions show that a fixed deployment delay prevents explosion in the specified delayed-feedback models, that power-law diversion of effort shifts the growth threshold, and that in a deterministic oversight race a fixed allocation share does not change the asymptotic regime, except at a critical equality of exponents. Numerical illustrations, which are not empirical forecasts, show how timing and detectability depend on the granularity of improvements.

##### **Copying Is Not Grounding: Training Faithful Long-Form RAG with Authored-Sentence Credit**
2610.18442v1 by Hoang Pham, Duc Anh Vu, Duy Tung Doan, Cong-Duy T Nguyen, Anh Tuan Luu

In search engines and assistants, long-form RAG answers can mislead readers with claims their sources do not support. Faithfulness rewards, the share of supported sentences, train models toward fewer such claims. We show that these rewards are hacked by copying. A sentence copied from the passages is supported almost by construction, so trained models learn to copy rather than ground what they write. Common defenses only move the hack: a KL or copy penalty leaves copying in place or trades it for shorter, emptier answers and refusals. GRPO, the standard RL algorithm, makes it worse by giving every sentence its answer's advantage, reinforcing copied and grounded sentences alike. We propose Authored-Sentence Credit (ASC) , which fixes both by moving GRPO's reward and advantage from the answer to the sentence level. The reward credits only sentences that are authored rather than copied, say more than the question, and are supported by the passages. The advantage compares each sentence with every sentence sampled for the question, so one that earns no credit ranks last and is never reinforced. Across four models and two in-domain, three held-out corpora, only ASC lowers the error rate of authored sentences and writes more supported ones in every setting, by 38% and 46% at the median under held-out evaluators, giving readers more grounded information. It takes none of these shortcuts, and two LLM judges comparing whole answers prefer no baseline to the base model but rate ASC's answers on par with it. We conclude that a faithfulness reward should credit what a model writes, not what it copies.

##### **Estimating Surprisal from the Speech Signal**
2610.18439v1 by Zachary Hopton, Samuel Kiegeland, Francesco Ignazio Re, Patrick Haller, Matthew B. Winn, Ryan Cotterell, Eleanor Chodroff

Although language is predominantly spoken, empirical tests of surprisal theory rely almost entirely on written text. In practice, surprisal is estimated using language models (LMs) trained on text and applied to transcriptions, creating a text bottleneck that limits evaluation of the theory in the spoken modality. We remove this bottleneck by estimating surprisal directly from the speech signal using a speech LM. This raises a key question: should surprisal be defined over realization-specific acoustic inputs or over abstract linguistic units? Since a single unit can have many distinct acoustic realizations, each assigned a different probability by the model, defining surprisal over abstract units is difficult. To address this, we introduce an importance sampling estimator that approximates the probability of an abstract unit by marginalizing over realizations generated by a text-to-speech model. Using pupillometry data from a listening study, we evaluate surprisal theory in this text-free setting at multiple levels of granularity. Obtaining surprisal from several speech LMs, we consistently find that surprisal estimated via importance sampling better predicts pupil dilation than realization-specific surprisal for words. We also find that surprisal values for sub-lexical units are predictive of pupil dilation. While we find that surprisal can be estimated without recourse to text, and that marginalizing over distinct realizations of words is more predictive of lexical processing effort, surprisal as a whole is not a strong predictor of change in pupil size, regardless of its source.

##### **STAMP as a topology-aware molecular language for fragment-level learning and editing**
2610.18438v1 by Yuxuan Ren, Jiatong Li, Ziqiao Meng, Yatao Bian

Molecular fragments provide chemically meaningful units, but existing molecular languages rarely make their locations and connections explicit for editing. Here we introduce STAMP (Sequential Tree Attachment-based Motif-Preserving representation), a topology-aware molecular language that connects fragment-level representation learning with precise structural editing. STAMP separately encodes motif identity, attachment sites and tree topology, making fragments reusable across contexts and subtrees directly accessible. Learned representations capture motif similarity and recurring transformations, and lower activity-cliff prediction error on 23 of 30 targets versus a matched SMILES encoder. Compatible subtree operations support scaffold-preserving optimization, ranking first among six methods on 15 of 23 benchmark objectives. A deterministic compiler enables frozen language models to act on indexed fragments, improving molecular editing, optimization, transformation transfer and change identification by 9 to 56 percentage points over plain SMILES. Together, these results establish an executable molecular language in which learned chemical units remain directly operable during design.

##### **How Well Do LLMs Perform in Peer Review? Evidence from Finance and Accounting**
2610.18435v1 by Hai Lu, Yichen Luo, Luping Yu

Referees increasingly use large language models (LLMs), while finance and accounting journals disagree about whether to allow it. We ask which part of a referee's job the models can do. GPT-4o, whose training data end before the 2024 conference season, referees the papers presented at the 2024 AFA and NBER finance meetings, and we compare its verdicts with publication in the top-three finance journals over the next two years. Papers the model scores one standard deviation higher are 44\% more likely to be published, net of author reputation. The result is not driven by look-ahead bias, as the model does not recall the outcomes. The information comes from its judgements of correctness and exposition. Its judgements of originality and significance carry none, and a referee guide that stresses importance does not help. The split between correctness and contribution holds for accounting papers and for a newer model, GPT-5. LLMs can check whether research is right, but not whether it matters. The technical check can go to the model, while the judgement of contribution stays with people.

##### **FedGraph: Defending Federated Large Language Model Fine-Tuning Against Backdoor Attacks via Graph-Based Aggregation**
2610.18430v1 by Xi Chen, Chunyi Zhou, Rui Zeng, Xiaogang Xu, Zhe Liu, Shouling Ji

Federated fine-tuning of large language models (LLMs) allows multiple clients to collaboratively adapt models without sharing raw data, offering strong privacy benefits. However, this setting is highly vulnerable to backdoor attacks, in which malicious clients manipulate their updates to induce targeted behaviors while maintaining normal performance on benign inputs.
  In this work, we make a key observation specific to federated LLM fine-tuning: malicious clients tend to exhibit strong consistency in their model updates, whereas benign clients produce more diverse and dispersed updates. This structural difference is not well captured by existing defense methods, which typically rely on direct anomaly detection in high-dimensional parameter space and therefore struggle in the presence of entangled LLM updates.
  Motivated by this observation, we propose FedGraph, a graph-based aggregation framework that explicitly models relationships among client updates. By representing clients as nodes in a dynamically constructed graph and extracting structural features that characterize their relative positions and interactions, FedGraph derives compact client representations that effectively separate malicious and benign behaviors. These representations are then used to identify and suppress malicious contributions during aggregation.
  Extensive experiments demonstrate that FedGraph significantly improves backdoor defense. It consistently reduces attack success rates to below 10% while achieving high detection accuracy (95.5% on average) and a low false positive rate (2.33%), substantially outperforming existing federated learning defenses.

##### **Reliable Memory from Unstable Growth: Curating Self-Written Agent Playbooks with Randomized Experiments**
2610.18427v1 by Hoang Pham, Cong-Duy Nguyen, Dong Le, Ponhvoan Srey, Anh Tuan Luu

Persistent textual memory lets language-model agents reuse lessons from past tasks to improve on future multi-step tasks without retraining. Systems such as ACE and Dynamic Cheatsheet keep it as a playbook: lessons the agent writes after each task and reads in its prompt. A key challenge is to decide which lessons improve later tasks. Current systems leave the decision to the agent itself, which makes performance unstable: the same procedure can write a playbook that helps or one that falls up to 69.6 points below no playbook. These findings motivate valuing lessons by their measured effect on task success rather than by the agent's judgment. We propose Randomized Block Curation (RBC), which measures this effect with a randomized experiment on practice tasks. It groups related lessons into blocks large enough to move success measurably and, on each practice task, includes or leaves out every block at random. Random inclusion makes tasks with and without a block comparable, so the gap in their success estimates the block's effect. RBC keeps the blocks that help and, since the same procedure can write a helpful or a harmful playbook, grows several and serves whichever curated, whole or no playbook scores best on fresh practice episodes. Across two memory systems, three models and three benchmarks, RBC raises average success to 66.9, against 55.5 with the whole playbook, 54.1 with no playbook and 53.8 when the agent's own judgment picks the lessons to keep.
